(5.21)

We now prove that the contraction of Corolla differential with the corresponding term in
\wedge_{(i,j)\,\in\,T}\,d\,\ln\,x_{ij}
generates
{\cal M}_{\gamma_{T}}^{\mu_{1}\,\dots\,\mu_{n}}(p_{1},\,\dots,\,p_{n})
. As discussed previously, the vertices of
\gamma_{T}
are ordered as follows. we define
i
-th vertex of
\gamma_{T}
as
v_{i}
. Then,

\displaystyle v_{i}\,=\,(e_{1,i},e_{i+1},e_{1,i+1})

\displaystyle v_{1}\,<\,v_{2}\,<\,\dots\,<\,v_{n-3}\,<\,v_{n-2}

(5.22)

Recall that at
v_{n-2}
all the edges are incoming and on any other vertex
v^{T}_{i}
,
e_{1,i},e_{i+1}
are incoming and
e_{1,i+1}
is outgoing.
The cyclic ordering on the three edges at
v^{T}_{i}
is then given by

\displaystyle e_{1,i}\,<\,e_{i+1}\,<\,e_{1,i+1}\,\textrm{and cyclic ordering}

(5.23)

We denote lorentz index on any(internal or external) edge
e_{v}
at
v
as
\mu_{e_{v}}
.
The Corolla differential can now be written as,

\displaystyle\hat{C}^{(0)}_{T}\,

\displaystyle=\,\prod_{i=1}^{n-2}\,\hat{C}_{{v_{i}^{T}}}

\displaystyle=\,\prod_{i=1}^{n-2}\,\sum_{I=1}^{3}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\eta^{\mu_{e_{v_{i}}^{I-1}}\,\mu_{e_{v_{i}}^{I+1}}}\,\sum_{\pm}\,\pm\,\epsilon_{e_{v_{i}}(I\,\pm\,1)}\,\left(\,\xi_{e_{v_{i}}(I\,\pm\,1)}^{2}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{e_{v_{i}}(I\,\pm\,1)}^{\mu_{e_{v_{i}}(I)}}}\,\right)

(5.24)

where, we recall that
\epsilon_{e_{v}}\,=\,\pm\,1~,

depending on whether the half edge
e_{v}
is outgoing (incoming) at the vertex
v
.

The contraction of
{\cal P}_{n}^{T}
with
\wedge_{(i,j)\,\in\,T}\,d\ln\,x_{ij}
produces a sum of terms which can be classified according to number of poles
\frac{1}{\xi_{1i}^{2}}
. A specific term which has no poles at
\{\,\xi_{(i,j)}^{2}\,|\,|(i,j)|=n_{\textrm{marked}}\,\}
can be represented as a
n_{\textrm{marked}}
graph, as shown in (
5.29
). Marked edges arise as follows. For concreteness let us zoom in on the action of a pair of Corolla differentials on the d
\ln
form.

In the rest of this section, we denote the Lorentz index on the chord
(1,i-1)
as
\mu_{\overline{i}}
.

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{n}\,\xi_{k}^{2}\,\dots\,\hat{C}_{(\overline{i},i,\overline{i+1})}\,\hat{C}_{(\overline{i+1}i+1\overline{i+2})}\,\dots\,\frac{1}{\prod_{j=1}^{n}\xi_{i}^{2}}\,\frac{1}{\xi_{1,i+1}^{2}}\,\dots

(5.25)

Where
\dots
to the left and right of Corolla differentials supress the remaining Corolla operators and the
\dots
to the right of
\frac{1}{\xi_{1,i+1}^{2}}
indicate the remaining propagators in the d-
\ln
form.

The action of the highlighted Corollas can be immediately evaluated and produce precisely one term which has no pole at
x_{1,i+1}=\xi_{1,i+1}^{2}=0
.

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{n}\,\xi_{k}^{2}\,\dots\,\hat{C}_{(\overline{i},i,\overline{i+1})}\,\hat{C}_{(\overline{i+1}i+1\overline{i+2})}\,\dots\,\frac{1}{\prod_{j=1}^{n}\xi_{i}^{2}}\,\frac{1}{xi_{1,i+1}^{2}}\,\dots\,\rightarrow\,\frac{1}{2}\,(\eta\,\wedge\,\eta)^{\mu_{\overline{i}}\mu_{\overline{i+2}}\mu_{i}\mu_{i+1}}\,\dots

(5.26)

We will denote such a term pictorially by marking the
(1,i+1)
chord as shown in (
5.27
).

\vdots
1
2
i
i+1

(5.27)

\ldots
1
2
i
i+1
\ldots
n-1
n

(5.28)

On the other hand, the same Corolla pair also produce a term which has a simple pole at
\xi_{1,i+1}^{2}\,=\,0
.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{x_{i,i+1}}\,V^{\mu_{\overline{i}}\mu_{i}\mu_{\overline{i+1}}}\,:=\,\frac{1}{x_{1,i+1}}\,\left(\,\eta^{\mu_{\overline{i+1}}\mu_{i}}\,\xi_{\overline{i+1}}^{\mu_{\overline{i}}}\,-\,\eta^{\mu_{\overline{i}}\mu_{\overline{i+1}}}\,\xi_{\overline{i+1}}^{\mu_{i}}\,\right)\left(\,\eta^{\mu_{\overline{i+1}}\mu_{\overline{i+2}}}\,\xi_{\overline{i+1}}^{\mu_{i+1}}\,-\,\eta^{\mu_{i+1}\mu_{\overline{i+1}}}\,\xi_{\overline{i+1}}^{\mu_{\overline{i+2}}}\,\right)

(5.29)

Pictorially we denote this term by marking vertices adjacent to the chord
(1,i+1)
. To summarise, the effect of Corolla differential on the
d\,\ln
form results in a sum of terms which have either marked vertices or marked edges. Several features of these markings for tree-level graphs are self evident but a reader interested in explicit proofs of these statements can refer to
[
2
]
for detailed proofs.

In light of the above discussion, the end result of the contraction
\langle{\cal P}_{n-3},\Omega_{n-3}\rangle
can be structurally understood in terms of a combinatorial structure that we refer to as marked triangulation
T_{\textrm{marked}}
.

Marked Triangulations

Given any triangulation
T
of an
n
-gon with
n-3
chords, we will consider a set of triangulations in each of which a subset of these chords are colored red and a subset of cells of
T
are marked with Blue. All the chords and cells which are not in these subsets remain un-colored and the colorings are required to satisfy the following conditions.
15
15

15

Every graph obtained by action of Corolla on a three regular graph is marked in the sense described below
[
2
]
. As each tri-valent graph is dual to a triangulation, we refer to each triangulation dual to a marked graph as marked triangulation.

1.

No two red chords can be adjacent to each other.

2.

If a chord
(i,j)
is red, then the two cells adjacent to it are uncolored.

3.

If a cell is blue, then all the boundaries of this cell (which are chords or external edges of the
n
-gon) are uncolored.

4.

As proved in
[
2
]
, the result of Corolla differential is a direct sum over marked triangulations such that total number of red and blue markings satisfy the following constraint:

\displaystyle n_{B}+2n_{R}\,=\,n-2~,

(5.30)

where
n_{R}
is the number of (non-adjacent) red chords and
n_{B}
is the number of blue cells.
16
16

16

Hence in a marked triangulation based on
T
, either a cell is colored and if it un-colored then exactly one of it’s edges is colored.
Look at
10
for the case of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

X_{13}
X_{35}
X_{25}
X_{24}
X_{14}
\mathcal{A}_{2}
1
2
3
4
5

Figure 10
:
Layered
associahedron
\mathcal{A}_{2}
: Each vertex corresponds to an equivalence class of triangulations.

Given a pair
(n_{B},n_{R})
that satisfy eqn.(
5.30
), we denote the set of marked triangulations based on
T
as
T_{\textrm{marked}}^{n_{R},n_{B}}
and the set of corresponding graphs as
\gamma_{T}^{n_{R},n_{B}}
.

For the vertex of
A_{n-3}
labelled by
T
, the scalar obtained via contraction of the multi-vector field with the
d\,\ln
form
\wedge_{(i,j)\,\in\,T}\,d\ln\,x_{ij}
can be written as a sum over
\oplus_{n_{B}=0}^{n-2}T_{\textrm{marked}}^{n_{R},n_{B}}
as follows.

\displaystyle S_{n_{R}}\,=\,\{\,(\,((i^{1}_{1},j^{1}_{1}),\,\dots\,(i^{1}_{n_{R}},j^{1}_{n_{R}})\,),\dots,\,(\,(i^{K}_{1},j^{K}_{1})\,\dots\,(i^{K}_{n_{R}},j^{K}_{n_{R}})\,)\,\}

(5.31)

be the set of all possible subsets of
n_{R}\,\geq\,1
red-chord configurations. As an example, consider
n=6
and let us consider a triangulation

\displaystyle T\,=\,\{\,(1,3),(1,4),(4,6)\,\}

(5.32)

Then
0\,\leq\,n_{R}\,\leq\,2
, and,

\displaystyle S_{n_{R}=1}

\displaystyle=\,\{\,(13),(14),(46)\,\}

\displaystyle S_{n_{R}=2}

\displaystyle=\,\{\,\big(13,46\big)\,\}

(
5.33
) depicts all such possible markings on chords for
n=6
.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

In each such subset in
S_{n_{R}}
, each red chord
c_{r}
is adjacent to a pair of cells
\triangle_{c_{r}}^{-},\triangle_{c_{r}}^{+}
with clock wise orientation inside each cell. Thanks to the orientation on the edges of the graph dual to
T
, one has an ordering on the two cells that are adjacent to
c
and without loss of generality we label the cells such that

\displaystyle\triangle_{c_{r}}^{-}\,<\,\triangle_{c_{r}}^{+}\,\forall\,c_{r}\,\in\,S_{n_{R}}

(5.34)

We also recall that with each cell we assign a triplet of Lorentz indices which are assigned to all edges of the polygon. For external edges,
e_{1},\dots\,e_{n}
these indices are
\mu_{1},\,\dots\,\mu_{n}
and for all the chords in
T
we assign indices
\mu_{c_{1}},\,\dots,\,\mu_{c_{n-3}}
.
We denote boundaries of each cell
\triangle
as
(\partial\triangle)_{I}|1\leq\,I\,\leq\,3
.
Marking a chord
c
implies that after the contraction
\frac{1}{x_{c}}
will be replaced by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,(\eta\,\wedge\,\eta)^{\mu_{(\partial\triangle_{c}^{-})_{1}}\mu_{(\partial\triangle_{c}^{-})_{2}}\mu_{(\partial\triangle_{c}^{+})_{2}}\mu_{(\partial\triangle_{c}^{+})_{1}}}

(5.35)

Thus finally, contribution of set of all marked triangulations with
n_{R}
red chords to the contraction is the function,

\displaystyle S^{\mu_{1}\,\dots\,\mu_{n}}_{T_{\textrm{marked}}^{n_{R},n_{B}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip-43.36243pt\sum\limits_{\hskip-3.61371pt(c_{1},\,\dots,\,c_{n_{R}})\,\in C_{n_{R}}}\hskip-3.61371pt\prod\limits_{\,c\,\in\,(c_{1},\dots,\,c_{n_{R}})}\frac{1}{2}\,(\eta\,\wedge\,\eta)^{\mu_{(\partial\triangle_{c}^{-})_{1}}\mu_{(\partial\triangle_{c}^{-})_{2}}\mu_{(\partial\triangle_{c}^{+})_{2}}\mu_{(\partial\triangle_{c}^{+})_{1}}}\hskip-36.135pt\prod\limits_{\triangle\,\in\,T|\,\partial\triangle\,\cap\,(c_{1},\dots\,c_{n_{R}})\,=\,0}\hskip-36.135ptV_{\triangle}^{\mu_{(\partial\triangle)_{1}}\mu_{(\partial\triangle)_{2}}\mu_{(\partial\triangle)_{3}}}

(5.36)

where

V_{\triangle}^{\mu_{(\partial\triangle)_{1}}\mu_{(\partial\triangle)_{2}}}
is defined in eqn.(
5.29
).

Hence the resulting scalar obtained by summing over all the vertices of
A_{n-3}
is a scalar in
{\cal K}_{n}^{\textrm{os}}
,

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{n}^{\mu_{1}\,\dots\,\mu_{n}}\,=\sum\limits_{T}\!\!\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{R}\,,\,n_{B}\\
n_{R}+2n_{B}=n-2\end{subarray}}\!\!\!S^{\mu_{1}\,\dots\,\mu_{n}}_{T_{\textrm{marked}}^{n_{R},n_{B}}}.

(5.37)

We can pull back
{\cal M}_{n}^{\mu_{1}\,\dots\,\mu_{n}}
to the on-shell Kinematic space that includes planar kinematic variables as well as scalars constructed out of
n
polarisations vectors and
\{p_{1},\,\dots,\,p_{n}\}
. The resulting function is the on-shell
n
point gluon amplitude
{\cal M}_{n}((\epsilon_{1},p_{1}),\,\dots,\,(\epsilon_{n},p_{n}))
.

5.2
On Gauge Invariance of
{\cal M}_{n}
.

How does action of Corolla differential guarantee that result is a gauge invariant object? A rigorous answer to this question was provided in
[
2
]
. The proof explored the relationship between BRST invariance of Feynman integrals in YM theory with homology of graph complexes,
[
36
]
. This is a rich subject in it’s own right and we refer the reader to
[
2
,
37
]
for a detailed exploration.
17
17

17

Readers not interested in the homological aspects of Corolla action can safely skip this section as our subsequent computations do not depend on these aspects. However the importance of these results should also not be ignored. That the homological proofs guarantee that action of Corolla differential on a
\phi^{3}
amplitude leads to a function which is gauge invariant and unitary at all orders in perturbation theory.

More in detail, it was shown that at the graph theoretic level, action of Corolla differential generates element of a graph homology as defined in
[
36
]
. Although this result holds for a graph with arbitrary number of cycles, we illustrate it for the simplest case of a planar tri-valent graph with no cycles.

Let
\chi_{e}
be an “edge-marking operator" for any
e\,\in\,E(\gamma_{n})
which is defined as follows.

\displaystyle\chi_{e}\,\gamma_{n}

\displaystyle=0\quad\textrm{if $e$ is adjacent to any other marked edge or a marked vertex.}

\displaystyle=\,\gamma_{n}^{e\,\rightarrow\,e_{\textrm{m}}}

(5.38)

where
e_{\textrm{m}}
indicates a marked edge.
Let

\displaystyle\chi=\sum_{e\in E(\gamma)}\chi_{e}

It is then clear that the sum over all the graphs obtained from
\gamma_{n}
and which contain at least one marked edge can written as,

\displaystyle\oplus\,\gamma_{n}^{\textrm{marked}}\,=\ e^{\chi}\,\gamma_{n}

(5.39)

One can now define a graph differential
S
as sum over two differentials
s_{1},s_{2}
. These operators may look rather formal and we will attempt to give a physical picture for them below. But first we simply define them following
[
2
]
.

In order to define such a differential, it is crucial that
E(\gamma)
be ordered. These differentials are defined as,

\displaystyle s_{1}\,\gamma\,

\displaystyle:=\,\sum_{e\,\in\,E(\gamma)}(-1)^{|e^{\prime}<e|,e^{\prime}\textrm{marked}}\,\gamma_{\textrm{marked}}(e\,\rightarrow\,\cancel{e})

(5.40)

\displaystyle s_{2}\,\gamma

\displaystyle:=\,(-1)^{|E_{m}\cup E_{\cancel{e}}|}\,\sum_{e\in E_{\textrm{m}}}\,(-1)^{\{|e^{\prime}>e_{m}|e^{\prime}\in E_{m}\}}\,\sigma_{e}\gamma_{\textrm{marked}}

(5.41)

\displaystyle\text{with}\qquad\sigma_{e}\gamma_{\textrm{marked}}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\textrm{marked}}(e\,\rightarrow\,\cancel{e})

(5.42)

It can now be checked that for
S=s_{1}+s_{2}
,

\displaystyle S^{2}\,\gamma

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle Se^{\chi}\,\gamma\,

\displaystyle=\,0

(5.43)

And thus
S
is a homological operator and
e^{\chi}(\gamma)\,\in\,H_{\star}(S)
. The action of
s_{1},s_{2}
involved a marking
\cancel{e}
distinct from the action of
\chi
. Intuitively,
\cancel{e}
can be understood as collapsing
e
to obtain quartic vertex. And thus action of
s_{1}
can be understood as collapsing an unmarked edge into four valent vertex and
s_{2}
action can be understood as identifying a marked edge with a four valent vertex. The fact that
e^{\chi}\gamma
generates graph homology is then necessary to ensure that for every graph with only three valent vertex, there is the appropriate number of graphs with quartic vertices consistent with gauge invariance.
We end this section with a remark.

•

For a given
\gamma
, one can simply choose an ordering once and for all but in our case, the ordering has to be preserved under mutation.
As we have seen previously, the “associative structure" encapsulated by combinatorial associahedron precisely equips each tri-valent graph with such an ordering.

6
Transmutation as inverse of Corolla differential

In a beautiful paper,
[
33
]
, a map was derived which maps the color ordered (tree-level) Yang-Mills amplitude
{\cal A}_{\textrm{YM}}
to the bi-adjoint
\phi^{3}
amplitude with identical color ordering in the two color- channels. The map between the two theories was through a differential operator (known as transmutation) in the space of all scalar invariants spanned by
p_{i}\cdot p_{j},\epsilon_{i}\cdot p_{j},\epsilon_{i}\cdot\epsilon_{j}
. Intuitively, transmutation operators undo the Corolla action! Given a specific color ordering for the Yang-Mills amplitude, the transmutation is a (product of) differential operators in the space spanned by
(\,p_{i}\cdot p_{j},\epsilon_{i}\cdot p_{j},\epsilon_{i}\cdot\epsilon_{j}\,)
such that

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{n}\,{\cal A}^{\textrm{co}}_{\textrm{YM}}(1,\dots,n)\,=\,{\cal A}_{\phi^{3}}^{\textrm{co}}(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})

(6.1)

Definition of
{\cal T}_{n}
can be found in
[
33
]
and will not be relevant for our purposes. The super-script on both the amplitudes stand for color-ordering.

As
{\cal A}_{n}((\epsilon_{1},p_{1}),\dots,(\epsilon_{n},p_{n}))
is a scalar in the “enlarged" Kinematic space parametrized by the planar kinematic variables and
(\epsilon_{i}\cdot p_{j},\epsilon_{i}\cdot\epsilon_{j})|_{1\leq\,i<j\leq\,n}
, we can now ask if there exists a transmutation-like operation that maps this function to
\Omega_{n-3}
in
{\cal K}_{n}
. In the present scenario however, such a map is rather easy to construct.

The sum over marked triangulations lead us to the following decomposition of the on-shell amplitude (see eqn.(
5.37
).),

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{\textrm{YM}}_{n}=\,\sum_{T}\frac{n_{T}}{\prod_{(ij)\in T}X_{ij}}+\sum_{n_{R}\neq\,0}\,\dots

(6.2)

where the first term is the contribution of
n_{R}=0
obtained by contracting the three point vertices and multiplying the result with the valuation of
\phi^{3}
amplitude over
\gamma_{T}
. The numerator factor
n_{T}
is a function in the enlarged kinematic space.

\displaystyle n_{T}=n_{T}(\{X_{ij},\epsilon_{i}\cdot p_{j},\epsilon_{i}\cdot\epsilon_{j}\})

(6.3)

Transmutation which maps this amplitude back to the d-log form is in fact rather simple to write down. Let
Y_{ij}=\frac{1}{X_{ij}}\,\forall\,1\,\leq\,i<j-1\,\leq\,n-2
.

\displaystyle\Upsilon\,:=\,\sum_{T}(-1)^{\sigma(T)}\,\wedge_{(ij)\in\,T}d\ln X_{ij}\,\Upsilon_{T}

(6.4)

where

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{T}\,=\,\frac{1}{n_{T}}\prod_{(mn)\in T}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial Y_{mn}}

(6.5)

We note that any term in the Yang-Mills amplitude which arises from a marked triangulation corresponding to
T
but with
n_{R}\,\neq\,0
is annihilated by the differential
\prod_{(mn)\in T}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial Y_{mn}}
. In essence,
\Upsilon
impliments the transmutation operation defined in
[
33
]
. However it does so at the level of individual vertices of the associahedron so as to generate the d-log form as opposed to the
\phi^{3}
amplitude. Of course, as is well known, the bi-adjoint amplitude is simply the pull back of d log form onto the ABHY associahedron in
{\cal K}_{n}
.

7
From
\hat{D}_{n}
polytope to one loop integrand of Yang-Mills amplitude

In this section we extend the above construction to one loop integrands of gauge theories. That is, we show that the contraction of the d
\ln
form induced by the
\hat{D}_{n}
polytope with the MVF
{\cal P}_{n}^{1-l}
maps the bi-adjoint amplitude to the Yang-Mills amplitude. The MVF in this case is defined using the Corolla polynomial associated to graphs with one cycle. This polynomial was reviewed in section (
4.3
), and is a difference of two polynomials with the relative sign signifying the contribution of the ghost loop. (See eqn. (
4.18
) for details.)

\displaystyle{\cal P}_{n}^{1-l}\,=\,\sum_{PT\,\in\,V(\hat{D}_{n})}\,(-1)^{PT}\,\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}[\,\hat{C}^{(0)}_{PT}-\hat{C}^{(1)}_{PT}\,]\,\prod_{IJ\,\in\,PT}\,\prod_{i=1}^{n}\,\frac{1}{x_{i}}\,\partial_{x_{IJ}}

(7.1)

We will now argue that
\langle\,{\cal P}_{n}^{1-l},\,\Omega_{n}^{1-l}\,\rangle
is the gauge invariant integrand for 1 loop gluon amplitude for Yang Mills theory. Just as action of the differential
\hat{C}^{(0)}
generate 3 point and 4 point gluon vertices, the action of
\hat{C}^{(1)}
“transmutes" a gluon loop into a ghost loop which restores the gauge invariance at the integrand level. This new contribution at one loop is in fact due to the 1 ghost-2 gluon vertex. We give a few examples of action of Corolla vector field on
\hat{D}_{n}
canonical form and argue that sum over tadpole graphs in bi-adjoint scalar amplitude cancel.

The contraction
\langle P_{n}^{1-l},\Omega_{n}^{1-l}\,\rangle
can then be written as,

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mu_{1},\,\dots\,\mu_{n}}_{1-l}(p_{1},\dots,\,p_{n},l)\,=\,\sum_{PT\,\in\,V(\hat{D}_{n})}\,\hat{C}_{PT}^{\mu_{1}\,\dots\,\mu_{n}}\,\prod_{c\,\in\,PT}\,\frac{1}{x_{c}}

(7.2)

where

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{PT}\,=\,\hat{C}^{(0)}_{PT}\,-\,\hat{C}^{(1)}_{PT}

(7.3)

At the outset, the action of Corolla on
d\ln
form appears as complicated as simply evaluating tedious Feynman diagrams. However some intriguing simplifications occur due to (1) cancellation in the Corolla polynomial thanks to the cycle polynomial
C^{(1)}
and (2) the sum over all the vertices of
\hat{D}_{n}
polytope wherein each Feynman diagram is repeated twice. The “primitive" diagrams whose Corolla polynomials generate Corolla differentials for all one loop graph can be divided into polynomials associated to tree-level graphs, a tadpole vertex, a wheel graph with
n\,\geq\,2
external legs. The corresponding Corolla polynomials can be immediately evaluated. Recall that
a_{h^{I}_{e}}\,|\,I\,\in\,\{1,2\}
is the variable which labels one of the two half edges
h^{1}_{e},h^{2}_{e}
which belong to the edge
e
and which are such that

\displaystyle h^{1}_{e}\,=\,(e,b(e)),\quad h^{2}_{e}=(e,f(e))

(7.4)

Then,

\displaystyle C_{\textrm{tadpole}}(a_{e},a_{e^{-}},a_{e^{+}})\,

\displaystyle=\,a_{e}

\displaystyle\hskip-50.58878ptC_{\textrm{wheel, n=2}}

\displaystyle=(\,a_{e}+a_{h^{1}_{e^{-}}}+a_{h^{1}_{e^{+}}}\,)(\,a_{e^{\prime}}+a_{h^{2}_{e^{-}}}+a_{h^{2}_{e^{+}}})-(a_{h^{1}_{e^{-}}}+a_{h^{1}_{e^{+}}})\,(a_{h^{2}_{e^{+}}}+a_{h^{2}_{e^{-}}})

\displaystyle=\,a_{e}a_{e^{\prime}}+a_{e}(a_{h^{2}_{e^{-}}}+a_{h^{2}_{e^{+}}})+a_{e^{\prime}}(a_{h^{1}_{e^{-}}}+a_{h^{1}_{e^{+}}})

\displaystyle C_{\textrm{wheel, n=3}}=\,a_{e_{1}}a_{e_{2}}a_{e_{3}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i<j<k|\textrm{cyclic}}[a_{e_{i}}a_{e_{j}}(a_{e_{k}^{-}}+a_{e_{k}^{+}})\,+\,a_{e_{i}}(a_{e_{j}^{-}}+a_{e_{j}^{+}})(a_{e_{k}^{-}}+a_{e_{k}^{+}})]

(7.5)

The labels on half edges are depicted in (
7.6
), (
7.7
) and (
7.8
).

\displaystyle\gamma_{\textrm{tadpole}}

\displaystyle=\quad\hbox to68.69pt{\vbox to68.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 68.48643pt\lower-34.6288pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(7.8)

Before diving into explicit computation involving the contraction of Corolla MVF with
\Omega_{n}^{1-l}
, we derive two important structural results.

1.

Given two one loop graphs
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
with tadpole vertices labelled by the chords
Y_{c},\,\tilde{Y}_{c}
the action of the corresponding Corolla differentials are related by

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{\gamma_{2}}\prod_{\textrm{prop}\in\gamma_{2}}\,\frac{1}{x_{\textrm{prop}}}+\hat{C}_{\gamma_{1}}\prod_{\textrm{prop}\in\gamma_{1}}\,\frac{1}{x_{\textrm{prop}}}=0

(7.9)

2.

Action of Corolla differential on a labelled graph without a tadpole vertex is independent of the orientation of the loop momenta. This implies that action of corolla differential on two vertices of
\hat{D}_{n}
polytope which are “mirror images" of each other and which do not contain chords of the type
(i,i)
are equal.

The first result can be proved using explicit computation. For concreteness we consider
n=4
but the results can be trivially extended
\forall\,n
.

To be specific, we consider the following four vertices of
\hat{D}_{4}
polytope. Dual graphs to these four vertices are forward limits of tree-level 6 point graphs with following ordering of external momenta. We denote these graphs as
\gamma_{i}^{13\,\textrm{t}}
, depicted in (
7.10
).

Then, we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}^{13\,\textrm{t}}\rightarrow\,(\,p_{1},p_{2},-l,l,p_{3},p_{4}\,)

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}^{13\,\textrm{t}}\,\rightarrow\,(\,p_{1},p_{2},l,-l,p_{3},p_{4}\,)

\displaystyle\gamma_{3}^{13\,\textrm{t}}\,\rightarrow\,(\,p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4},l,-l\,)

\displaystyle\gamma_{4}^{13\,\textrm{t}}\,\rightarrow\,(\,p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4},-l,l\,)

(7.11)

Using eqn.(
7.5
), we can write down the action of Corolla on
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
as follows.

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{\gamma_{i}}=\hat{C}_{v_{1}}\,\hat{C}_{v_{2}}\hat{C}_{w_{i}}\hat{C}_{v_{3}}

(7.12)

where

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{w_{1}}\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\,\eta^{\mu_{+}\mu_{-}}\left(\,-(l^{+})^{2}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial l_{+}^{\alpha}}+(l^{-})^{2}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial l_{-}^{\alpha}}\,\right)

(7.13)

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{w_{2}}\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\,\eta^{\mu_{+}\mu_{-}}\left(\,-(l^{-})^{2}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial l_{-}^{\alpha}}+(l^{+})^{2}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial l_{+}^{\alpha}}\,\right)

(7.14)

We thus see that

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{w_{1}}+\hat{C}_{w_{2}}=0

(7.15)

This result can be trivially extended to any pair of
n
-point graphs with two tadpole subgraphs which differ only in the orientation of the loop momenta proving assertion in eqn.(
7.9
). As a result, in the contraction of
\langle{\cal P}_{n}^{1-l},\Omega_{n}^{1-l}\,\rangle
we simply drop all those vertices of
\hat{D}_{n}
polytope which correspond to graphs with tadpole vertices.

An alert reader may worry that as the set of vertices of
\hat{D}_{n}
always contain a pair of vertices with the same underlying graph but with loop momenta running clockwise and counter-clockwise respectively, perhaps the contraction of
{\cal P}_{n}^{1-l}
with the d
\ln
form trivially vanishes. We will now show that this is not the case and for all the vertices whose underlying graphs do not contain tadpole vertices, switching the direction of loop momenta keeps the Corolla action invariant. Once again, the simplest example is sufficient to display this general property. We thus consider
n=2
and radiative correction to the propagator, as shown in (
7.16
).
18
18

18

The super-script indicates that these are bubble graphs.

Let us assign the following four vector labels to all the edges of the two graphs.

\displaystyle\gamma^{\textrm{b}}=\ \ \ \hbox to100.41pt{\vbox to54.22pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 50.20206pt\lower-27.31998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ \ \ \rightarrow\,(p_{1},l_{-},l_{+},p_{2})

(7.16)

The corresponding two vertices of
\hat{D}_{2}
can be obtained from
\gamma^{\textrm{b}}
by substituting

\displaystyle v_{1}

\displaystyle:=(\,p_{1}=p,p_{2}=-p,l_{-}=l,l_{+}=p_{1}+l\,)\quad\textrm{or}

\displaystyle v_{2}

\displaystyle:=(\,p_{1}=p,p_{2}=-p,l_{-}=-(p_{1}+l),l_{+}=-l\,)

(7.17)

after the action of Corolla differentials on the two graphs.

The corolla polynomial for the underlying graph is given in eqn.(
7.5
) using which the corresponding differentials can be immediately written down. We will spare the reader from the relatively straightforward algebra which can be verified and simply write down the action of the Corolla differential on
\gamma^{\textrm{b}}
.

\displaystyle\hat{C}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\gamma^{\textrm{b}}}\,=\,\frac{D^{2}+8}{4}\,\sum_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}=\pm}\left(\eta\,\eta^{\prime}\,l_{\eta}^{2}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial l_{\eta}^{\mu_{1}}}\ l_{\eta^{\prime}}^{2}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial l_{\eta^{\prime}}^{\mu_{2}}}\,\right)

(7.18)

It can now be immediately verified that,

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{2}\,p_{i}^{2}\ \hat{C}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\,\frac{1}{p_{j}^{2}}\,\frac{1}{l_{-}^{2}l_{+}^{2}}\,\big|_{v_{1}}=\prod_{i=1}^{2}\,p_{i}^{2}\ \hat{C}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\,\frac{1}{p_{j}^{2}}\,\frac{1}{l_{-}^{2}l_{+}^{2}}\,\bigg|_{v_{2}}

(7.19)

where
v_{1},v_{2}
are the two momenta assignments given in eqn.(
7.17
). Hence we finally have the result of the contraction of Corolla induced MVF with the d log form defined by
\hat{D}_{2}
polytope.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\langle\,{\cal P}_{2}^{1-l},\Omega_{2}^{1-l}\,\rangle\,=\,\prod_{i=1}^{2}p_{i}^{2}\hat{C}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\,\frac{1}{p_{j}^{2}}\,\frac{1}{l_{-}^{2}l_{+}^{2}}\,\bigg|_{v_{1}}

(7.20)

As the vertices in
V(\hat{D}_{n})
that contain tadpole subgraphs do not contribute in the contracted scalar, we simply need to analyse the action of Corolla differential on rest of the vertices. In this case, the action can be cleanly captured by defining marked pseudo-triangulation which is exactly analogous to the concept of marked triangulation introduced in section (
5
). As the idea is exactly analogous, we simply illustrate it diagrammatically. In the figure
11
, we show how the action of the Corolla differential can be realised as transforming a pseudo-triangulation into a set of marked pseudo-triangulation where either vertices are marked or edges which are not adjacent to marked vertices are marked.
19
19

19

Of course the crucial difference in the explicit expression of marked triangles from marked pseudo-triangles is that the former corresponds to a three gluon vertex, whereas the latter corresponds to a combination of the three-gluon vertex and a two ghost one gluon vertex if any one of the boundaries of the pseudo-triangle is an arc that ends at the puncture.

\hat{D}_{2}

Figure 11
:
\hat{D}_{2}
polytope with each vertex corresponding to a set of marked pseudo-triangulations.

7.1
Explicit Example: planar one loop two points

We will now illustrate the results obtained above with some explicit examples. We work out the simplest one-loop amplitude case, planar two-point, in detail. The
\hat{D}_{2}
polytope, is a two-dimensional polygon as there are two internal propagators. There are six edges (faces) corresponding to six curves, and six vertices corresponding to six graphs. Figure
7
depicts the g-vectors and triangulations for this case.

Accounting for the doubling of graphs, we restrict ourselves to the
\sum t_{i}\leq 0
region. As a result, we have three graphs to account for:

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{22.05344pt}{-57.983pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 30.52 -80.23)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(7.21)

We can readily write the canonical form for this as follows:

\displaystyle\Omega=\differential X_{l_{1}}\wedge\differential X_{p_{\Sigma}}-\differential X_{l_{1}}\wedge\differential X_{l_{2}}+\differential X_{p_{\Sigma}}\wedge\differential X_{l_{2}}+\ldots

(7.22)

\ldots
refer to the rest of the three triangulations in
\sum t_{i}\geq 0
region.

Let us write the differential operator valued polyvector
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{1-l}
. The momentum labels are in accord with (
7.21
).

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{2}\,\xi_{2}^{2}}\,\mathcal{P}_{2}^{1-l}

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt=\left[\eta^{\nu\mu_{2}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{\xi_{2}}+\frac{p_{\Sigma}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{p_{\Sigma}}\right)^{\mu_{1}}+\eta^{\mu_{1}\nu}\left(-\frac{p_{\Sigma}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{p_{\Sigma}}-\frac{\xi_{1}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{\xi_{1}}\right)^{\mu_{2}}+\eta^{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{\xi_{1}}-\frac{\xi_{2}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{\xi_{2}}\right)^{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\times(4-2D)\left(\frac{l_{1}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{l_{1}}\right)_{\nu}\ \frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{2}\,\xi_{2}^{2}}\ \partial_{X_{l_{1}}}\wedge\partial_{X_{p_{\Sigma}}}

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt-\bigg\{\left[\eta^{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}\left(-\frac{l_{2}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{l_{2}}-\frac{l_{1}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{l_{1}}\right)^{\mu_{1}}+\eta^{\nu_{2}\mu_{1}}\left(-\frac{\xi_{1}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{\xi_{1}}+\frac{l_{2}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{l_{2}}\right)^{\nu_{1}}+\eta^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\left(\frac{l_{1}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{l_{1}}+\frac{\xi_{1}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{\xi_{1}}\right)^{\nu_{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left[\eta^{\mu_{2}}{}_{\nu_{2}}\left(\frac{l_{2}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{l_{2}}+\frac{\xi_{2}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{\xi_{2}}\right)_{\nu_{1}}+\eta_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}\left(-\frac{l_{1}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{l_{1}}-\frac{l_{2}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{l_{2}}\right)^{\mu_{2}}+\eta_{\nu_{1}}{}^{\mu_{2}}\left(-\frac{\xi_{2}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{\xi_{2}}+\frac{l_{1}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{l_{1}}\right)_{\nu_{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(\frac{l_{2}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{l_{2}}\right)^{\mu_{1}}\left(\frac{l_{1}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{l_{1}}\right)^{\mu_{2}}-\left(\frac{l_{2}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{l_{2}}\right)^{\mu_{2}}\left(\frac{l_{1}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{l_{1}}\right)^{\mu_{1}}\bigg\}\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{2}\,\xi_{2}^{2}}\ \partial_{X_{l_{1}}}\wedge\partial_{X_{l_{2}}}

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt+\left[\eta^{\mu_{2}\nu}\left(-\frac{p_{\Sigma}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{p_{\Sigma}}-\frac{\xi_{2}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{\xi_{2}}\right)^{\mu_{1}}+\eta^{\nu\mu_{1}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{\xi_{1}}+\frac{p_{\Sigma}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{p_{\Sigma}}\right)^{\mu_{2}}+\eta^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{\xi_{2}}-\frac{\xi_{1}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{\xi_{1}}\right)^{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\times(4-2D)\left(\frac{l_{2}^{2}}{2}\,\partial_{l_{2}}\right)_{\nu}\ \frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{2}\,\xi_{2}^{2}}\ \partial_{X_{p_{\Sigma}}}\wedge\partial_{X_{l_{2}}}

(7.23)

Note that the ghosts have been accounted for. We have omitted the three ‘mirror image’ contributions from the
\sum t_{i}>0
region.

Now, we can obtain the one-loop integrand by contracting the polyvector (
7.23
) with the canonical form (
7.22
) as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{P}_{2}^{1-l}\,,\,\Omega_{2}^{1-l}\rangle

\displaystyle=(4-2D)\big(l_{2}-l_{1}\big)_{\nu}\left[\eta^{\nu\mu_{2}}\big(-\xi_{2}-p_{\Sigma}\big)^{\mu_{1}}+\eta^{\mu_{1}\nu}\big(p_{\Sigma}+\xi_{1}\big)^{\mu_{2}}+\eta^{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}}\big(-\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}\big)^{\nu}\right]\frac{1}{l_{1}^{2}\,p_{\Sigma}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg(\left[\eta^{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}\big(l_{2}+l_{1}\big)^{\mu_{1}}+\eta^{\nu_{2}\mu_{1}}\big(\xi_{1}-l_{2}\big)^{\nu_{1}}+\eta^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\big(-l_{1}-\xi_{1}\big)^{\nu_{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times\left[\eta^{\mu_{2}}{}_{\nu_{2}}\big(-l_{2}-\xi_{2}\big)_{\nu_{1}}+\eta_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}\big(l_{1}+l_{2}\big)^{\mu_{2}}+\eta_{\nu_{1}}{}^{\mu_{2}}\big(\xi_{2}-l_{1}\big)_{\nu_{2}}\right]\bigg)\frac{1}{l_{1}^{2}\,l_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+(1-D)\frac{1}{l_{1}^{2}}\,\eta^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}+(1-D)\frac{1}{l_{2}^{2}}\,\eta^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad-\left(l_{2}{}^{\mu_{1}}\,l_{1}{}^{\mu_{2}}+l_{2}{}^{\mu_{2}}\,l_{1}{}^{\mu_{1}}\right)\,\frac{1}{l_{1}^{2}\,l_{2}^{2}}

(7.24)

At this point, we can impose momentum conservation, and this gives us the integrand of the one-loop two-point amplitude. Since we have not imposed momentum conservation and on-shell conditions yet in (
7.24
), these graphs can be embedded in any bigger graphs.

In (
7.24
), the first term contains the tadpole contributions. Note that there are no contact vertices appearing due to tadpole graphs. The second term is the trivalent gluon bubble graph, corresponding to none of the lines marked. The next two terms in the penultimate line are the diagrams with a contact vertex, corresponding to
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
marked respectively. The last term is the ghost contribution.

Note that the doubling of terms,
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
being marked, are consistent with the usual Feynman diagrams. Figure
12
depicts the underlying structure of the one-loop, two-point contact interaction graph. The middle diagram, with a line in the tadpole diagram marked, is zero. The other two terms are precisely
l_{i}
being marked.

Figure 12
:
Underlying structure of the contact vertex for a ’wart’ diagram under usual Feynman rules

8
Curve integral formula for gluons

The Corolla differential defined in the kinematic space is directly inspired by the definition given by KSVS of the graph differential
\hat{C}_{\gamma}^{\textrm{sch}}
in the parametric space spanned by the Schwinger parameters. We review this seminal construction in section
8.1
.
\hat{C}_{\gamma}^{\textrm{sch}}
maps the top form in the parametric space which integrates to contribution of
\gamma
to
\phi^{3}
amplitude to a top form which integrates to contribution of
\gamma
to Yang-Mills (and in general gauge theory) amplitude.

On the other hand, in the recent works
[
3
,
4
]
, the authors discovered and proved the so-called
curve integral formula
for
\phi^{3}
amplitudes, (See section
8.2
for a brief review). Clearly, it is natural to consider a “fusion" of these two ideas and obtain a curve integral formula for gluon amplitudes. Results in earlier section should convince the reader that this should indeed be possible and in this section we give explicit curve integral formula for tree-level and (planar) 1-loop
n
point gluon amplitude. As both the curve integral formula and the Corolla graph differential is well defined at arbitrary order in perturbation theory, generalisation of our results to higher loops should be immediate and will be pursued in the future.

8.1
Review of Corolla graph differential

In
[
2
]
, Corolla polynomials on a fixed trivalent graph
\gamma
were represented as differential operators-valued meromorphic maps in the parametric space co-ordinatized by the Schwinger parameters.
20
20

20

Ordinarily in the amplitude computations, one assigns a Schwinger parameter
t_{e}
to any internal edge
e
of a
k
-valent graph both of whose end points are
k
-valent vertices. However, following
[
2
]
, the parametric space we consider is
{\bf R}^{|E_{\textrm{int}}|+|E_{\textrm{out}}|}
with Schwinger parameters also labelling external edges.
A remarkable all loop parametric representation of Yang-Mills amplitude was then a result of the action of such a Corolla differential on
\phi^{3}
Schwinger integrand,
[
39
]
. We now review this result.

Let
{\cal U}_{\gamma},\,{\cal F}_{\gamma}
be the First and Second Symanzik graph polynomials obtained from
\gamma
. We denote the contribution of a
\gamma
to the (parametric representation) of
\phi^{3}
amplitude as
\textrm{Val}_{\phi^{3}}(\gamma)
.

\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{\phi^{3}}(\gamma)\,:=\,\int\,\prod_{i=1}^{E}dt_{i}\,\left(\frac{\pi^{L}}{{\cal U}_{\gamma}}\right)^{\frac{D}{2}}\,e^{-\,\frac{{\cal F}_{\gamma}}{{\cal U}_{\gamma}}}

(8.1)

where
{\cal U}_{\gamma},\,{\cal F}_{\gamma}
are the first and second Symanzik polynomials which are generated by the set
{\cal T}_{1}
of all the 1-spanning trees and set
{\cal T}_{2}
of all the 2 spanning trees of
\gamma
respectively.
21
21

21

If
\gamma
has no cycles, then
{\cal U}_{\gamma}:=1
and
{\cal F}_{\gamma}:=\,\sum_{I\,\in\,\textrm{prop}}t_{I}P_{I}^{2}
,

\displaystyle{\cal U}_{\gamma}\,

\displaystyle=\,\sum_{T_{1}\,\in\,{\cal T}_{1}}\,\prod_{e\,\notin\,T_{1}}\,t_{e}

\displaystyle{\cal F}_{\gamma}\,

\displaystyle=\,\sum_{T_{2}=T_{2}^{\prime}\Pi T_{2}^{\prime\prime}\,\in\,{\cal T}_{2}}\,\prod_{e\notin\,T_{2}}t_{e}\bigg(\sum_{e^{\prime}\in T_{2}^{\prime}}q_{e^{\prime}}\bigg)^{2}

(8.2)

where
\sum_{e^{\prime}\in T_{2}}q_{e^{\prime}}^{\mu}
is the physical momenta flowing into
\gamma
from
T_{2}^{\prime}
. For more details on the parametric representation using Symanzik polynomials, we refer the reader to
[
40
,
41
,
42
,
43
]
.

The Corolla differential introduced by KSVS in
[
2
]
requires a minor re-writing of
\textrm{Val}_{\phi^{3}}(\gamma)
. We label all the edges of the graph with independent four-vector labels
\xi_{e}\,\forall e\in E_{\textrm{in}\cup\textrm{out}}
.
\textrm{Val}_{\phi^{3}}
can then be re-written as,

\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{\phi^{3}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip-36.135pt\int_{0}^{\infty}\,\prod_{i=1}^{E}dt_{i}\,\prod_{j=1}^{|E_{\textrm{out}}|}\xi_{j}^{2}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\,ds_{j}\left(\frac{\pi^{L}}{{\cal U}_{\gamma}}\right)^{\frac{D}{2}}\,e^{-\,\frac{{\cal F}_{\gamma}(\{\xi_{e^{\prime}}\})_{e^{\prime}\in E_{\textrm{in}}}}{{\cal U}_{\gamma}}-\sum_{m=1}^{|E_{\textrm{out}}|}s_{m}\xi_{m}^{2}}|_{\xi_{e}=p_{e}\,\forall e\in E_{\textrm{out}}\ ;\ \xi_{e^{\prime}}=q_{e^{\prime}}\forall e^{\prime}\in E_{\textrm{in}}}

(8.3)

where
q_{e^{\prime}}
was introduced in eqn.(
8.2
).

Action of Corolla graph differential introduced in
[
2
]
on
e^{-\frac{{\cal F}}{{\cal U}}}
leads to the Yang-Mills integrand in the parametric space
\textrm{Val}_{\textrm{YM}}
. The discovery of the Corolla polynomial as a generator of graph homology (which leads to gauge invariant perturbative expansion of Yang-Mills amplitude) is a rather beautiful chapter in parametric representation of gauge theory Feynman graphs. For a nice exposition of these ideas we refer the reader to
[
38
]
and for application of the idea to QED see
[
39
]
).

We will denote the Corolla graph-differential in the parametric space
\hat{C}_{\gamma}^{\textrm{sch}}
to distinguish it from
\hat{C}_{\gamma}
which is defined in the kinematic space. Parametric space is the positive quadrant in
{\bf R}^{|E_{\textrm{in}}|+|E_{\textrm{out}}|}
where
E_{\textrm{in(out)}}
is the set of internal (external) edges of
\gamma
all of which begin and end in a trivalent vertex. This space is co-ordinatized by the Schwinger parameters
\{t_{1},\dots,\,t_{|E_{\textrm{in}}|},s_{1},\dots,s_{|E_{|\textrm{out}|}}\}
.

As we have already defined a Corolla differential
\hat{C}_{\gamma}
in the kinematic space,
\hat{C}_{\gamma}^{\textrm{sch}}
defined by KSVS can be understood via the following replacement in
\hat{C}_{\gamma}
. Given the Corolla differential
\hat{C}_{\gamma}
on the kinematic space.

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{\gamma}(\vec{t},\vec{s})\,:=\,\hat{C}_{\gamma}|_{\xi_{e^{\prime}}^{2}\,\rightarrow\,\frac{1}{t_{e^{\prime}}}\ ,\ p_{e}^{2}\,\rightarrow\,\frac{1}{s_{e}}}

(8.4)

The parametric representation of the Yang-Mills amplitude is then given by the following master formula. Given a
\gamma
, let
Let
{\cal E}\,\subset\,E_{\textrm{int}}
be a subset of internal edges no two of which share a common vertex. Let
{\cal E}^{c}
be the compliment of
{\cal E}
in
E_{\textrm{int}}
. For an
n
-point tri-valent graph with no cycles, cardinality of any such
{\cal E}
lies between

\displaystyle 0\,\leq\,|{\cal E}|\,\leq\,\lceil{\frac{n}{2}\rceil}

(8.5)

Let
f:{\bf R}^{|E_{\textrm{tot}}|}\,\rightarrow\,{\bf R}
be a function of the parametric space over internal as well as external edges which has (1) simple poles at most on
t_{e}|e\,\in\,{\cal E}_{k}
for some subset
{\cal E}_{k}
with cardinality
k
and, (2) is regular on
E_{\textrm{tot}}-{\cal E}_{k}
.
22
22

22

That is,
f_{{\cal E}_{k}}
is regular in the limit
t_{e^{\prime}}\,\rightarrow\,0\,\forall e^{\prime}\in{\cal E}_{k}^{c}
.

We can now consider

\displaystyle\textrm{Res}(f)

\displaystyle=\,\sum_{k\geq\,0}\,\sum_{{\cal E}_{k}\subset E_{\textrm{int}}}\,\prod_{e\in{\cal E}_{k}}\,\oint\,dt_{e}f

\displaystyle=\,\sum_{k\geq\,0}\sum_{{\cal E}_{k}\subset E_{\textrm{int}}}f_{{\cal E}_{k}}

(8.6)

where

\displaystyle f_{{\cal E}_{k}}:{\bf R}^{|{\cal E}_{k}^{c}|}\,\rightarrow\,{\bf R}

(8.7)

Hence one can now evaluate the natural pairing

\displaystyle\int_{{\bf R}^{|{\cal E}_{k}^{c}|}}\wedge_{e^{\prime}}dt_{e^{\prime}}\textrm{Res}(f_{{\cal E}_{k}})

(8.8)

Given any function
f:{\bf R}^{|E_{\textrm{tot}}|}\,\rightarrow\,{\cal R}
which has at most simple poles on subsets of edges no two of which share a vertex, one can define

\displaystyle\textrm{Reg-Res}\,f:=\sum_{k\geq\,0}\,\int_{{\bf R}^{|{\cal E}_{k}^{c}|}}\bigwedge_{e^{\prime}}\differential t_{e^{\prime}}\textrm{Res}(f_{{\cal E}_{k}})

(8.9)

Thus, we have

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{\textrm{YM}}_{n}(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\gamma}\,\textrm{Color}_{\gamma}\,\frac{1}{{\cal U}_{\gamma}^{\frac{D}{2}}}\,\textrm{Res-Reg}(\,\hat{C}_{\gamma}^{\textrm{sch}}e^{-\frac{{\cal F}(\xi_{e_{1}},\dots,\xi_{e_{|E|_{\textrm{int}}}})}{{\cal U}}-\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\textrm{int}}}s_{i}\xi_{i}^{2}})\,\bigg|_{\xi_{i}=p_{i},\,\xi_{e}=K_{e}\,\forall\,e\in E_{\textrm{int}}}

(8.10)

where
\textrm{Color}_{\gamma}
is the color factor. This striking result holds at all loops and in fact has been used to obtained parametric representation of a wide class of gauge theories including gauge theories coupled to matter from parametric integrals of
\phi^{3}
amplitudes. We refer the reader to
[
38
,
39
]
for application of this formula to obtain a wider class of gauge theory amplitudes from
\phi^{3}
amplitude.

We now note that although the Residue in the (Reg-Res) formula computes the residue of the meromorphic function
f_{\gamma}(t_{e_{1}}\,\dots,\,t_{e_{|E_{\textrm{int}}|}})
on simple poles over a subset of
mutually disjoint
(internal) edges, we could equally well consider set of subets of internal edges (as opposed to demanding that the edges be mutually disjoint.) This is because the Corolla action results in a integrand in the parametric space which never has poles on two adjacent edges. This is simply because given a sequence of set of edges
e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}
such that either

(1) all three of them meet in a vertex or,

\displaystyle e\cap e^{\prime}\neq\,0\,\textrm{and}\,e^{\prime}\cap e^{\prime\prime}\neq\,0

Then action of Corolla differential on a function that depends on
t_{e},t_{e^{\prime}},t_{e^{\prime\prime}}
, will always result in a meromorphic function which in the first case have no double poles involving two of
(t_{e},t_{e^{\prime}},t_{e^{\prime\prime}})
and in the second case can only have double pole in
(t_{e},t_{e^{\prime\prime}})
but not in
(t_{e},t_{e^{\prime}})
or
(t_{e^{\prime}},t_{e^{\prime\prime}})
. This proves the above assertion.

The formula derived by KSVS “spins up"
\textrm{Val}_{\phi^{3}}(\gamma)
to obtain
\textrm{Val}_{YM}(\gamma)
and of course one can sum over all the 3-valent graphs to obtain the full amplitude at a given loop order. However, in
[
3
,
4
]
, the authors proved that the generic (single trace as well as multi-trace color factors)
n
point
L
loop colored
\phi^{3}
amplitude in the parametric space can also be expressed in terms of the so-called first and second
surface
Symanzik polynomials. The topology of the underlying surface is fixed by the number of boundary points, genus and number of marked points, the latter corresponding to the degree of non-planarity in the dual graph.

In light of the fact that individual Feynman diagrams can be evaluated using graph Symanzik polynomials, this is a striking result. In a nut-shell, any
n
-point,
L
loop, multi-trace amplitude is an integral of a top form in the parametric space where the form is completely fixed by the
{\bf g}
-vector fan, (see section
3.2
for a review of
g
vector fans for an
n
point graph with
L\,\leq\ 1
and no marked points) and the coordinates of the Kinematic space. Hence, the
{\bf g}
-vector fan, which gave a unique realization of the positive geometries also defines a top form in the
R^{|E|}
that leads to parametric representation of
\phi^{3}
amplitude. This formula was called the curve integral formula in
[
3
]
. As KSVS’s original discovery of the Corolla differential was in obtaining the Schwinger parametrization of a diagram for gluon amplitude from a
\phi^{3}
diagram, it is a rather natural question to ask if we can “fuse" these two seminal ideas together and obtain curve integral formula for gluon amplitude. This is to be expected from results in the previous section in the kinematic space, but the details of the resulting “curve integral formula for gluon amplitude" perhaps also have an intrinsic value. In the rest of the paper, we derive such a formula for tree-level and one loop amplitudes and provide some explicit examples. As we observe, the Corolla differential which appears inside the curve integrand depends non-trivially on the
{\bf g}
vector fan.

We will analyze the tree level amplitude in section
8.3
and work out some examples in the following section. In section
8.4
, we present the curve integral formula at planar one loop. We present some rudimentary examples in the following section.

8.2
Review of the curve integral formula for scalars

In this section, we briefly review the seminal results derived in
[
3
,
4
]
for
\phi^{3}
amplitudes. Although the results derived in these papers are completely general in the sense that they generate a full
n
point amplitude at all orders in perturbation theory including non-planar diagrams, we will restrict ourselves to the parametric representation for tree-level and planar one loop
\phi^{3}
amplitudes which will generate corresponding Yang-Mills amplitude using a formula inspired from eqn.(
8.10
).

8.2.1
The central idea

The headlight functions are (piece-wise) linear forms on the vector space spanned by
{\bf g}
vectors and are labeled by the curves. They are fixed through the following defining equation.

\displaystyle\alpha_{c^{\prime}}(g_{c})=\delta_{cc^{\prime}}~.

(8.11)

In
[
3
,
4
]
, the authors present a beautiful algorithm to compute the headlight functions for arbitrary processes.

The definition of the headlight function dictates that
\alpha_{c}
‘turns on’ only in those cones which share positive span of
\vec{g}_{c}
as a common boundary. Tree-level color ordered massless
\phi^{3}
amplitude can now be written in a compact form as,

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{n}^{\phi^{3}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{|E_{\text{int}}|}}\differential^{|E_{\text{int}}|}\vec{t}\ e^{-\sum_{c}\alpha_{c}X_{c}}

(8.12)

where for
c=[i,j]
,
X_{c}=(p_{i}+\dots+p_{j-1})^{2}
and
|E_{\textrm{int}}|=n-3
.

Equipped with such a remarkably simple formula, it is easy to see how this reproduces the tree-level amplitude. This is essentially due to the fact that the fan spanned by set of all
{\bf g}
vectors is complete and essential.
23
23

23

A fan is complete if the
g
vectors span
{\bf R}^{n-3}
and is essential if the intersection of all the cones is the origin,
[
16
]
.
As a result, the above integral over
n-3
dimensional parametric space splits into sum over top dimensional cones each of which is bounded by (positive span) of
\{{\bf g}_{c}\}
over the curves which generate a complete triangulation of the
n
-gon.

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{n}^{\tr\phi^{3}}\,=\sum_{T}\,\int\differential^{n-3}\vec{t}\,e^{-\sum_{c\,\in\,T}\,\alpha_{c}X_{c}}

(8.13)

In a top-dimensional cone (indexed by a complete triangulation), the volume form can written in terms of the headlight functions as follows:

\displaystyle\differential^{n-3}\vec{t}\,\big|_{T}

\displaystyle=\bigwedge_{c\in T}\differential\alpha_{c}

(8.14)

\displaystyle\differential^{|E_{\text{int}}|}\vec{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{T}\bigwedge_{c\in T}\differential\alpha_{c}

(8.15)

As an example, for
n=5
and with a reference triangulation
T_{0}=\{13,14\}
the corresponding
g
-vector fan spans
{\bf R}^{2}
.

\displaystyle g_{13}=(1,0),\ \ \ g_{14}=(0,1),\ \ \ g_{25}=(-1,0),\ \ \ g_{35}=(0,-1),\ \ \ g_{24}=(-1,1)

(8.16)

and the headlight functions are shown in (
8.70
).
Using (
8.14
) in (
8.13
), we can integrate the curve integral formula in each cone:

\displaystyle\int_{T}\differential^{n-3}\vec{t}\ e^{-\sum_{c}\alpha_{c}X_{c}}=\int\,\differential^{n-3}\vec{t}\ e^{-\sum_{c^{\prime}\in T}\alpha_{c^{\prime}}X_{c^{\prime}}}\,=\prod_{c\in T}\frac{1}{X_{c}}

(8.17)

Thus, the integral over the entire fan gives us a sum over all cones, i.e., sum over all trivalent graphs, and we obtain the tree-level trivalent scalar amplitude.

The four-point tree-level example is quite instructive, as the
g
-vectors
g_{13},g_{24}
span
{\bf R}
and the headlight functions are precisely the heavyside functions:

\displaystyle g_{13}=1,g_{24}=-1

(8.18)

and

\displaystyle\alpha_{13}=t\,\theta(t)=\text{max}\big(0,t\big)~~,

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\alpha_{24}=-t\,\theta(-t)=\text{max}\big(0,-t\big)

(8.19)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential^{n-3}\vec{t}\ e^{-\sum_{c}\alpha_{c}\,X_{c}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential t\ e^{-\alpha_{13}\,X_{13}-\alpha_{24}\,X_{24}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential t\ e^{-t\,\theta(t)\,X_{13}}+\int_{-\infty}^{0}\differential t\ e^{t\,\theta(-t)\,X_{24}}=\frac{1}{X_{13}}+\frac{1}{X_{24}}~.

(8.20)

We now re-write the curve integral formula in the parametric space
{\bf R}^{|E_{\textrm{int}}|+|E_{\textrm{out}}|}
as follows. Let
s_{i}|i\,\in\,\{1,\,\dots,\,|E_{\textrm{ext}}|\}
be the set of additional parameters. Let

\displaystyle\alpha_{c}=\textrm{max}(0,s_{c})\,\forall\,c\,\in\,E_{\textrm{ext}}

(8.21)

Let
x_{c}
be independent real variables
\forall\,c\,\in\,E
.
The curve integral formula in eqn.(
8.12
) can be re-written as follows.

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{n}^{\tr\phi^{3}}(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})\,=\,\prod_{c^{\prime}\in E_{\textrm{ext}}}\,x_{c^{\prime}}\int_{{\bf R}^{n-3+n}}\,d^{n-3}\vec{t}\,d^{n}s\,e^{-\sum_{c}\alpha_{c}(\vec{t},\vec{s})x_{c}}\ \bigg|_{x_{c}=X_{c}}

(8.22)

\displaystyle X_{c}=0\,\forall\,c\,\in\,E_{\textrm{out}}

(8.23)

8.2.2
Planar one-loop integrated curve integrated formula

We now review the curve integral formula for planar
\tr\phi^{3}
amplitude at one loop,
[
3
,
4
]
. The parametric representation of
\textrm{Val}_{\phi^{3}}(\gamma)
is in terms of Symanzik polynomials which is obtained by writing the loop integrand in Schwinger representation and integrating over loop momenta. One of the beautiful corollaries of the curve integral formula is the discovery that the “graph theoretic definition of Symanzik graph-polynomials in terms of spanning trees in fact extends to curve integral formulae to all orders in perturbation theory. Specifically, at one loop, integrating over the loop momenta leads to a curve integral formula for one loop amplitude in terms of so-called surface Symanzik polynomials. we briefly review this result which will hopefully make the reader more acquainted with the formula. The structural ingredients involved in this derivation also help us in deriving curve integral formula for planar, one loop gluon amplitudes.

Let us recall that in the case of
n
external particles, there are
n(n+1)
number of curves which generate pseudo-triangulation of an
n
gon with a hole in the center. These are,
C_{ij}
,
S_{i}
and
S_{i}^{\prime}
. (See figures
5
and
4
.) The headlight functions corresponding to these curves were computed in
[
3
,
4
]
and can be written as,

\displaystyle C_{ik}

\displaystyle=i\,L\,t_{i}\,R\,t_{i+1}\,R\ldots R\,t_{k-1}L\,k~,

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\alpha_{C_{ik}}=f_{i,(k-2)}+f_{(i+1),(k-1)}-f_{(i+1),(k-2)}-f_{i,(k-1)}~.

(8.24)

\displaystyle S_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=iR\,\big(t_{i-1}L\,t_{i-2}L\,t_{i-3}L\ldots L\,t_{i-n}\,L\big)^{\infty}~,

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\alpha_{S_{i+1}^{\prime}}=-t_{i}+f_{i,(i+n-2)}-f_{(i+1),(i+n-1)}~.

(8.25)

\displaystyle f_{i,k}

\displaystyle=\textrm{max}(\big(t_{i}+t_{i+1}+\ldots+t_{k}\big)\ ,\ \big(t_{i+1}+t_{i+2}+\ldots+t_{k}\big)\ ,\ \ldots\ ,\ \big(t_{k-1}+t_{k}\big)\ ,\ t_{k}\ ,\ 0)

(8.26)

We can hence write the one-loop planar
n
-point scalar amplitude as follows:

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{n\,L=1}^{\tr\phi^{3}}=\int_{\sum t_{i}<0}\differential^{n}\vec{t}\int\differential^{D}\ell\ \exp\left(-\hskip-11.38092pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[1,n]\\
j\in[i+2,i+n]\end{subarray}}\hskip-11.38092pt\alpha_{C_{ij}}X_{ij}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{S_{i}^{\prime}}X_{S_{i}^{\prime}}\right)

(8.27)

We have labelled the loop momentum as
\ell
. Note that we have restricted the integral to the half of the parametric space bounded by
\sum t_{i}=0
, to account for the doubling of the graphs.
24
24

24

The co-dimension one hyper-plane is also called
\triangle
-plane in
[
3
,
4
]
.
The headlight functions for the spirals
S_{i}
vanish in the particular Schwinger region
\sum t_{i}<0
as can be verified from explicit computation. For the benefit of the reader, we recall that (figures
4
,
5
).

\displaystyle X_{ij}

\displaystyle=(p_{i}+\ldots+p_{j-1})^{2}\equiv(z_{j}-z_{i})^{2}~,

(8.28)

\displaystyle X_{S_{i}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=(-l_{i-1})^{2}\equiv(\ell+z_{i})^{2}~,

(8.29)

where we have defined the
z_{i}
, the dual coordinates to momenta,

\displaystyle p_{i}=z_{i+1}-z_{i}~,\qquad z_{n+1}=z_{1}.

(8.30)

The total momentum conservation of
p_{i}
becomes the translation symmetry of
z_{i}
. We can fix one of the
z_{i}
for concreteness as follows:

\displaystyle z_{1}

\displaystyle=0~,\quad z_{2}=p_{1}~,\quad z_{3}=p_{1}+p_{2}~,\quad\ldots\quad~,\quad z_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}p_{i}

(8.31)

\displaystyle l_{n}

\displaystyle=\ell~,\qquad l_{1}=\ell+p_{1}~,\quad\ldots\quad~,\quad l_{k}=\ell+\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}=\ell+z_{k+1}

(8.32)

Looking back at (
8.27
), we can carry out the loop integral to obtain:

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{n,L=1}^{\tr\phi^{3}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\sum t_{i}<0}\differential^{n}\vec{t}\exp\left(-\sum_{ij}\alpha_{C_{ij}}\,z_{ij}^{2}\right)\int\differential^{D}\ell\ \exp\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{S_{i}^{\prime}}(\ell+z_{i})^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\sum t_{i}\leq 0}\differential^{n}\vec{t}\ \left(\frac{-\pi}{\sum_{i}t_{i}}\right)^{D/2}\exp\left(-\sum_{ij}\alpha_{C_{ij}}z_{ij}^{2}-\frac{\left(\sum_{i}\alpha_{S_{i}^{\prime}}z_{i}\right)^{2}}{\sum_{i}t_{i}}-\sum_{i}\alpha_{S_{i}^{\prime}}z_{i}^{2}\right)~,

(8.33)

This is the explicit form for (
8.35
). Note that we made use of the following fact while simplifying the integral:

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\alpha_{S_{i}^{\prime}}=-\sum_{i}t_{i}~.

(8.34)

{\cal A}_{n}^{\phi^{3}\,1-l}
in eqn.(
8.33
) is in fact a parametric representation of the planar one loop amplitude terms of so-called surface Symanzik polynomials. It can be shown that the above formula can be written as,

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{n}^{\phi^{3}\,(L=1)}(\vec{x})\,=\,\int_{{\bf R}^{n}}\,\differential^{n}\vec{t}\,\left(\frac{\pi}{{\cal U}^{D/2}}\right)\,e^{-\,\frac{{\cal F}}{{\cal U}}\,+\sum_{c}\alpha_{c}x_{c}}

(8.35)

where given a surface
S
with
n
marked points on the boundary and an annulus in the center, the surface Symanzik polynomials are defined in terms of the headlight functions as follows. Let
{\cal S}_{1}
be the set of curves that cut
{\cal S}
into a disk and let
{\cal S}_{2}
be the set of all pair of curves that cut
S
into two disjoint disks.

\displaystyle{\cal U}

\displaystyle=\sum_{c\,\in\,S_{1}}\,\alpha_{c}

(8.36)

\displaystyle{\cal F}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(c,c^{\prime})\in{\cal S}_{2}}\alpha_{c}\alpha_{c^{\prime}}\,\left(\sum_{i}p^{\mu}\right)^{2}

(8.37)

As
\textrm{Val}_{\phi^{3}}
, one can trivially re-write these curve integral formulae in the same way eqn.(
8.1
) was re-written as
8.3
. Of course this makes no difference for
\phi^{3}
amplitude, but is central to the Corolla action.

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{n}^{\phi^{3}(L=1)}(\vec{x})\,=\,\prod_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}^{2}\,\int d^{n}\vec{t}\,\int_{-\infty}^{0}\,d^{n}\vec{s}\,\left(\frac{\pi}{{\cal U}^{\frac{D}{2}}}\right)\,e^{-\,\frac{{\cal F}}{{\cal U}}+\sum_{w}\alpha_{w}x_{w}}

(8.38)

where the sum in the exponent is over
all the curves
including the external edges for which the headlight functions are defined in eqn.(
8.21
).

Once again, the physical amplitude is obtained by substituting

\displaystyle x_{c}=X_{c}\,\forall\,c

(8.39)

Although we have restricted our review to the curve integral formulae for obtaining tree-level and (planar)
\phi^{3}
amplitudes, it is important to emphasize that the striking power of these formulae lie in their “universality". Namely the complete
\phi^{3}
amplitude at all orders in the perturbation theory including the non-planar contributions can be obtained using curve integral formulae.

The Corolla differential operators shall have dependence on the legs with ‘loop’ momenta as well. We need to morph (
8.33
) into an appropriate form such that all the internal and external legs’s momenta are explicitly present. Let us label the momenta corresponding to the curve
C_{ij}
as
\xi_{ij}
, and to the curve
S_{i+1}^{\prime}
as
-\xi_{i0}
. After imposing the momentum conservation, we shall identify
\xi_{ij}
with
z_{ij}
.
\xi_{i0}
shall appear in certain linear combinations only so that after imposing momentum conservation, we have only
z_{ij}
dependence. We make the following replacement:

\displaystyle z_{ij}\to\xi_{ij}~,\hskip 56.9055ptz_{i}\to(\xi_{i-1,0}-\xi_{n,0})

(8.40)

One can check that (
8.33
) upon this substitution becomes:

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{\phi^{3}}_{n}=\int_{\sum t_{i}\leq 0}\differential^{n}\vec{t}\ \left(\frac{-\pi}{\sum_{i}t_{i}}\right)^{D/2}\exp\left(-\sum_{ij}\alpha_{C_{ij}}\xi_{ij}^{2}-\frac{\big(\sum_{i}\alpha_{S_{i+1}^{\prime}}\,\xi_{i,0}\big)^{2}}{\sum_{i}t_{i}}-\sum_{i}\alpha_{S_{i+1}^{\prime}}\,\xi_{i,0}^{2}\right)\bigg|_{\,\xi_{C}=\text{phys}}

(8.41)

Including the external particles as well, we rewrite the curve integral formula appropriate for the action of Corolla:

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{\phi^{3}}_{n}

\displaystyle=\int\differential^{n}s\int_{\sum t_{i}\leq 0}\differential^{n}\vec{t}\ \left(\frac{-\pi}{\sum_{i}t_{i}}\right)^{D/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\times\exp\left(-\sum_{k\in\text{Ext}}s_{k}\,\xi_{k}^{2}-\sum_{ij}\alpha_{C_{ij}}\xi_{ij}^{2}-\frac{\big(\sum_{i}\alpha_{S_{i+1}^{\prime}}\,\xi_{i,0}\big)^{2}}{\sum_{i}t_{i}}-\sum_{i}\alpha_{S_{i+1}^{\prime}}\,\xi_{i,0}^{2}\right)\bigg|_{\,\xi_{C}=\text{phys}}~.

(8.42)

8.3
Curve integral for gluons at tree-level

We now derive the second primary result of this paper: The curve integral formulae for planar (color-ordered) gluon amplitude. We start with the tree-level amplitudes. In the next section, we will derive the curve integral formula for the planar one-loop gluon amplitude. Throughout this section, we will denote the Corolla differentials associated to a triangulation as
\hat{C}_{T}
instead of
\hat{C}^{(0)}_{T}
. As we only focus on deriving tree-level gluon amplitudes, we drop the super-script
(0)
from the Corolla differentials.

The starting point is a (trivial re-writing of) the tree-level curve integral formula for
\phi^{3}
amplitude given in eqn.(
8.22
). As we will show below, the corresponding curve integral formula in Yang-Mills theory involves action of Corolla differential which is a weighted sum over Corolla graph differentials.

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{YM}}_{n}\big[1,\ldots,n\big]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\,\in\text{ ext}}\xi_{i}^{2}\,\big(\text{Reg-Res}\big)\,\frac{\sum_{T}\left(\prod_{c\in T}\alpha_{c}\right)\hat{C}_{T}}{\sum_{T}\left(\prod_{c\in T}\alpha_{c}\right)}\ \exp\left(-\sum_{w}\alpha_{w}\,\xi_{w}^{2}\right)\bigg|_{\xi_{w}=p_{\text{phys}}}~.

(8.43)

The sum over chords
c\,\in T
is sum over all the dissection chords of the triangulation
T
and sum over
w
in the exponent include sum over all possible dissections of the
n
-gon as well as sum over external sides
[i,i+1]\,|\,1\leq\,i\,\leq\,n-1
of the
n
-gon. The differential
\hat{C}_{T}
was introduced in eqn.(
8.4
). In fact,
\hat{C}_{T}=\hat{C}_{\gamma(T)}
where
\gamma
is the tree-level planar graph dual to
T
.

We shall expand on this formula (including the (Res-Reg) operation) below. The (Reg-Res) procedure required to extract the specific curve-integrand is a generalisation of the procedure for a fixed graph. This procedure is explained in section (
8.3.1
) below.

The Corolla operator for a particular cone

Eqn.(
8.43
) simply reduces to sum over all the triangulations and hence equals
\sum_{\gamma}\textrm{Val}_{\textrm{YM}}(\gamma)
. This is simply due to the following identity.

\displaystyle\int d^{n-3}\vec{t}\,\frac{F_{T}(\vec{t})\,\prod_{c\in T}\,\alpha_{c}}{\sum_{T}\prod_{c^{\prime}\in T}\alpha_{c^{\prime}}}\,e^{-\,\sum_{c^{\prime\prime}}\alpha_{c^{\prime\prime}}x_{c^{\prime\prime}}}=\sum_{T}\,\int\,d^{n-3}\vec{t}\,F_{T}(\vec{t})\,e^{-\,\sum_{c\in T}\alpha_{c}x_{c}}

(8.44)

As a result, eqn.(
8.43
) can be re-written as,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{YM}}_{n}\big[1,\ldots,n\big]

\displaystyle=\,\prod_{i\,\in\text{ ext}}\xi_{i}^{2}\int\,d^{n-3}\vec{t}\,d^{n}\vec{s}\,\sum_{T}\big(\text{Reg-Res}\big)_{T}\ \hat{C}_{T}\ e^{-\sum_{C\in\text{int, ext}}\alpha_{C}\!(\vec{t})\,\xi^{2}_{C}}\ \bigg|_{\xi_{i,C}=p_{\text{phys}}}

\displaystyle=\,\sum_{\gamma}\textrm{Val}_{\textrm{YM}}(\gamma)~.

(8.45)

For the benefit of the reader, we recall that
x_{c}=\xi_{i}^{2}
for
c=[i,i+1]
.

The last equality follows from the identification of sum over
T
with sum over all the trivalent graphs. Of course for a single graph, the Corolla graph differential defined in KSVS is known, (eqn.(
8.4
) ). In light of the fact that the exponent
-t_{c}\xi_{c}^{2}
in the standard Schwinger parametrisation is replaced with
-\alpha_{c}x_{c}
in the curve integral formula. It is natural to consider the Corolla differential operator where
\frac{1}{t_{e}}
is replaced by
\frac{1}{\alpha_{c}}
.

We write the differential operator
\hat{C}_{v}
for a particular vertex
v
, with three external particles below for completeness:

\displaystyle\hbox to103pt{\vbox to62.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 56.05171pt\lower-31.09529pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}

{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 31.5 -31.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -47.24 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}

(8.46)

Note that the conventions for the orientation of vertices, etc. remain the same as in the earlier sections. We repeat that in all the graphs, the orientation of all the vertices shall be consistently the same, and the direction of momentum flow in the shared legs of any two graphs shall be the same.

8.3.1
The (Reg-Res) extraction

The result of Corolla graph differential in the Parametric space is a meromorphic functions which evaluates to
\textrm{Val}_{\gamma}(\textrm{YM})
after applying the (Res-Reg) procedure, (see eqns (
8.6
,
8.7
,
8.8
). This procedure hinges upon isolating poles of a graph dependent function
f_{\gamma}(t_{1},\dots,t_{|E_{\textrm{tot}}|})
over a subset of Schwinger parameters associated to set of internal edges which are mutually disconnected. However the curve integral formula treats space of all graphs democratically and hence we need to generalise eqn. (
8.9
) accordingly.

Our generalisation is based on the simple observation that the (Res-Reg) “extraction" is primarily based on the set of red chords (which are dual to marked edges of a graph obtained after action of Corolla differential) in a marked triangulation. In light of the discussion in the section
5
, it immediate follows that each of the colored chords is accompanied with a
1/\alpha_{C}
in the curve integrand which does not get cancelled by
\alpha_{C}
in the numerator once Corolla differential applies. This observation in conjunction with the fact that any generalisation of eqn(
8.9
), when restricted to a particular triangulation
T
should simply reduce to the Res-Reg formula for functions defined on
\gamma_{T}
(once we change the variables from
\alpha_{C\in T}\to t_{i}
) is sufficient to generalise the formula to the full curve integral. That is, our generalisation should be such that,

\displaystyle\big(\text{Reg-Res}\big)_{T}=\,(\textrm{Reg-Res})_{\gamma(T)}~,

(8.47)

We now write down the (Res-Reg) procedure for the curve integral. As the volume form in the parametric space can be written as, (see eqn. (
8.14
) )

\displaystyle\differential^{E}\vec{t}|_{T}=\bigwedge_{I\in T}\differential\alpha_{I}

(8.48)

we can write the (Reg-Res) as follows: Let
{\cal A}_{[1]}
be the set of all co-dimension one facets of the associahedron and let
\chi
be the set of all proper subsets of
{\cal A}_{[1]}
including an empty set. We note that a given
\chi
can also involve facets which are not adjacent to each other and hence these set of all such subsets is larger than (union over all the tri-valent planar graphs of) set of proper subsets of internal edges of a given graph. However as the Corolla differential will only poles over faces which are adjacent to each other, any subset which involve co-dimension one facets which do not share a common vertex of the associahedron will not contribute to the sum.

\displaystyle\big(\text{Reg-Res}\big)\,\rightarrow\,\sum_{\chi\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{[1]}}\int\,d^{n-3}\vec{t}\ d^{n}\vec{s}\,\prod_{c\,\in\,\chi}\delta(\alpha_{c}(\vec{t}))\,\underset{\{\alpha_{c\in(\mathcal{A}^{[1]}/\chi)\,\cup E_{\textrm{ext}}}\}}{\text{Regular}}\ \underset{\{\alpha_{c\in\chi}\}\to 0}{\text{Residue}}

(8.49)

The extraction formula may look rather convoluted but it can be verified that it simply reduces to eqn.(
8.9
) inside each cone in the parametric space. Several comments are in order.

•

As the action of Corolla never induces a pole in any of the Schwinger parameters
\{s_{1},\,\dots,\,s_{n}\}
, the (Res-Reg) extraction can be applied over the headlight functions associated to dissection chords of the
n
-gon and finally integrate the integrand with respect to
d^{n}\vec{s}
followed by multiplication with
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\,\xi_{i}^{2}
. This elementary observation helps us in writing detailed computations and will be used throughout subsequent sections where we work out several examples.

•

Examples given in sections (
8.3.2
,
8.3.3
) help with the concrete application of eqn.(
8.49
).

To summarize, combining eqns (
8.46
,
8.49
) we obtain the curve integral formula for color-ordered tree-level gluon amplitudes is given in eqn.(
8.45
).

8.3.2
Example: Four point tree level

Let us work out the
n=4
case in detail. There is only one internal edge for tree level
n=4
, so the g-vector fan lives in 1-dimensional space. There are two
curves
corresponding to two:
s
(
X_{13}=\xi_{13}^{2}
) and
t
(
X_{24}=\xi_{24}^{2}
) channel graphs.

The headlight functions are as follows:

\displaystyle\alpha_{13}=\text{max}\big(0,t\big)=t\,\theta(t)~,\qquad\alpha_{24}=-t+\text{max}\big(0,t\big)=-t\,\theta(-t)~.

(8.50)

Since the top dimensional cones are made up of single
g
-vectors, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{T}\left(\prod_{I\in T}\alpha_{I}\right)\hat{C}_{T}}{\sum_{T}\left(\prod_{I\in T}\alpha_{I}\right)}=\frac{\alpha_{13}\,\hat{C}_{(13)}+\alpha_{24}\,\hat{C}_{(24)}}{\alpha_{13}+\alpha_{24}}=\begin{cases}\hat{C}_{(13)}&t>0\\
\hat{C}_{(24)}&t<0\end{cases}

(8.51)

There are two complete triangulations, leading to the two graphs shown below:

\displaystyle\gamma_{(13)}\ \ :\quad

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{(13)}

\displaystyle=\left[\eta^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha_{1}}\,\partial_{\xi_{1}}-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{2}}\,\partial_{\xi_{2}}\right)^{\nu}+\eta^{\mu_{2}\nu}\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha_{2}}\,\partial_{\xi_{2}}-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{13}}\,\partial_{\xi_{13}}\right)^{\mu_{1}}+\eta^{\nu\mu_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha_{13}}\,\partial_{\xi_{13}}-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{1}}\,\partial_{\xi_{1}}\right)^{\mu_{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\times\left[\eta_{\nu}{}^{\mu_{4}}\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha_{13}}\,\partial_{\xi_{13}}+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{4}}\,\partial_{\xi_{4}}\right)^{\mu_{3}}+\eta^{\mu_{4}\mu_{3}}\left(-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{4}}\,\partial_{\xi_{4}}+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{3}}\,\partial_{\xi_{3}}\right)_{\nu}+\eta^{\mu_{3}}{}_{\nu}\left(-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{3}}\,\partial_{\xi_{3}}-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{13}}\,\partial_{\xi_{13}}\right)^{\mu_{4}}\right]

(8.53)

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{(24)}

\displaystyle=\left[\eta^{\mu_{1}}{}_{\nu}\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha_{1}}\,\partial_{\xi_{1}}+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{24}}\,\partial_{\xi_{24}}\right)^{\mu_{4}}+\eta_{\nu}{}^{\mu_{4}}\left(-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{24}}\,\partial_{\xi_{24}}-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{4}}\,\partial_{\xi_{4}}\right)^{\mu_{1}}+\eta^{\mu_{4}\mu_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha_{4}}\,\partial_{\xi_{4}}-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{1}}\,\partial_{\xi_{1}}\right)_{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle\times\left[\eta^{\nu\mu_{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha_{24}}\,\partial_{\xi_{24}}-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{2}}\,\partial_{\xi_{2}}\right)^{\mu_{3}}+\eta^{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha_{2}}\,\partial_{\xi_{2}}-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{3}}\,\partial_{\xi_{3}}\right)^{\nu}+\eta^{\mu_{3}\nu}\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha_{3}}\,\partial_{\xi_{3}}-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{24}}\,\partial_{\xi_{24}}\right)^{\mu_{2}}\right]

[1,2,3,4]
clockwise.

Finally, the (Reg-Res) term in this integrand is,

\displaystyle\big(\text{Reg-Res}\big)=\int\differential^{4}s_{i}\left[\left(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential t\ \underset{\alpha_{13}\,,\,\alpha_{24}}{\text{Regular}}\right)+\underset{\alpha_{24}}{\text{Regular}}\ \underset{\alpha_{13}\to 0}{\text{Residue}}+\underset{\alpha_{13}}{\text{Regular}}\ \underset{\alpha_{24}\to 0}{\text{Residue}}\right]

(8.57)

Here, we made use of the fact that there shall be no poles in
\alpha_{i}
for external legs. This simplified the contributions from external legs to (Reg-Res).

(8.59)

We have integrated the
s_{i}
, the Schwinger like parameters for external particles, which yielded us
1/\xi_{i}^{2}
, and in the presence of
\xi_{i}^{2}
gave us unity.

Note that as the usual Feynman rules dictate, the vertex factor for a contact interaction comes from three channels. As we see in (
8.65
), only two channels contribute to the planar amplitude.

8.3.3
n=5

The g-vector space is two-dimensional for
n=5
. There are five curves, hence five
g
-vectors, dual to the five faces (1-d edges) of the two-dimensional (pentagon) associahedron. We denote the five curves as
C_{k(k+2)}
carrying the momenta
(p_{k}+p_{k+1})
. There are five cones and hence five trivalent graphs, corresponding to the five vertices of the associahedron. All the triangulations are
ray-like
, so let us label them as
T(k)_{k\in[1,\ldots,5]}
in accord with figure (
8.60
).

T(k)\quad:
\xi_{k-2}
(\mu_{k-2})
\xi_{k-1}
(\mu_{k-1})
\xi_{k}~~(\mu_{k})
\xi_{k+2}
(\mu_{k+2})
\xi_{k+1}
(\mu_{k+1})
\xi_{(k-2)k}
(\nu_{1})
\xi_{(k+1)(k-2)}
(\nu_{2})

(8.60)

For the (
8.60
), we write down the Corolla differential operator:

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{T(k)}

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\eta^{\nu_{1}\mu_{k-2}}\left(-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k-2}}\,\partial_{\xi_{k-2}}-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{(k-2)k}}\,\partial_{\xi_{(k-2)k}}\right)^{\mu_{k-1}}+\eta^{\mu_{k-1}\nu_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha_{(k-2)k}}\,\partial_{\xi_{(k-2)k}}+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k-1}}\,\partial_{\xi_{k-1}}\right)^{\mu_{k-2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 247.53888pt+\eta^{\mu_{k-2}\mu_{k-1}}\left(-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k-1}}\,\partial_{\xi_{k-1}}+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k-2}}\,\partial_{\xi_{k-2}}\right)^{\nu_{1}}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\times\bigg\{\eta_{\nu_{1}}{}^{\mu_{k}}\left(-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k}}\,\partial_{\xi_{k}}+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{(k-2)k}}\,\partial_{\xi_{(k-2)k}}\right)_{\nu_{2}}+\eta^{\mu_{k}}{}_{\nu_{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha_{(k+1)(k-2)}}\,\partial_{\xi_{(k+1)(k-2)}}+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k}}\,\partial_{\xi_{k}}\right)_{\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle\hskip 184.9429pt+\eta_{\nu_{2}\nu_{1}}\left(-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{(k-2)k}}\,\partial_{\xi_{(k-2)k}}-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{(k+1)(k-2)}}\,\partial_{\xi_{(k+1)(k-2)}}\right)^{\mu_{k}}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\times\bigg\{\eta^{\mu_{k+1}\mu_{k+2}}\left(-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k+2}}\,\partial_{\xi_{k+2}}+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k+1}}\,\partial_{\xi_{k+1}}\right)^{\nu_{2}}+\eta^{\mu_{k+2}\nu_{2}}\left(-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{(k+1)(k-2)}}\,\partial_{\xi_{(k+1)(k-2)}}+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k+2}}\,\partial_{\xi_{k+2}}\right)^{\mu_{k+1}}

\displaystyle\hskip 184.9429pt+\eta^{\nu_{2}\mu_{k+1}}\left(-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k+1}}\,\partial_{\xi_{k+1}}+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{(k+1)(k-2)}}\,\partial_{\xi_{(k+1)(k-2)}}\right)^{\mu_{k+2}}\bigg\}

(8.61)

We can write the 5-point tree level YM amplitude as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{5}\big[1,\ldots,5\big]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{5}\xi_{i}^{2}\,\big(\text{Reg-Res}\big)\,\frac{\sum_{T}\left(\prod_{I\in T}\alpha_{I}\right)\hat{C}_{T}}{\sum_{T}\left(\prod_{I\in T}\alpha_{I}\right)}\ \exp\left(-\sum_{C}\alpha_{C}\,\xi_{C}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{5}\xi_{i}^{2}\,\big(\text{Reg-Res}\big)\,\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{5}\alpha_{(k-2)k}\,\alpha_{(k+1)(k-2)}\,\hat{C}_{T(k)}}{\sum_{k=1}^{5}\alpha_{(k-2)k}\,\alpha_{(k+1)(k-2)}}\exp-\sum_{i=1}^{5}\left(\alpha_{i}\,\xi_{i}^{2}+\alpha_{i(i+2)}\,\xi_{i(i+2)}^{2}\right)

(8.62)

Let us evaluate the action of Corolla differential operator
\hat{C}_{T}
on the exponential:

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{T(k)}\exp-\sum_{i=1}^{5}\left(\alpha_{i}\,\xi_{i}^{2}+\alpha_{i(i+2)}\,\xi_{i(i+2)}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\eta^{\nu_{1}\mu_{k-2}}\left({\xi_{k-2}}+{\xi_{(k-2)k}}\right)^{\mu_{k-1}}+\eta^{\mu_{k-1}\nu_{1}}\left(-{\xi_{(k-2)k}}-{\xi_{k-1}}\right)^{\mu_{k-2}}+\eta^{\mu_{k-2}\mu_{k-1}}\left({\xi_{k-1}}-{\xi_{k-2}}\right)^{\nu_{1}}\bigg\}e^{-\sum\ldots}

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg\{\eta^{\mu_{k}}{}_{\nu_{2}}\left(-{\xi_{(k+1)(k-2)}}-{\xi_{k}}\right)_{\nu_{1}}+\eta_{\nu_{2}\nu_{1}}\left({\xi_{(k-2)k}}+{\xi_{(k+1)(k-2)}}\right)^{\mu_{k}}+\eta_{\nu_{1}}{}^{\mu_{k}}\left({\xi_{k}}-{\xi_{(k-2)k}}\right)_{\nu_{2}}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg\{\eta^{\mu_{k+2}\nu_{2}}\left({\xi_{(k+1)(k-2)}}-{\xi_{k+2}}\right)^{\mu_{k+1}}+\eta^{\nu_{2}\mu_{k+1}}\left({\xi_{k+1}}-{\xi_{(k+1)(k-2)}}\right)^{\mu_{k+2}}+\eta^{\mu_{k+1}\mu_{k+2}}\left({\xi_{k+2}}-{\xi_{k+1}}\right)^{\nu_{2}}\bigg\}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\alpha_{(k+1)(k-2)}}\left(\eta^{\mu_{k}\mu_{k+2}}\,\eta_{\nu_{1}}{}^{\mu_{k+1}}-\eta^{\mu_{k}\mu_{k+1}}\,\eta_{\nu_{1}}{}^{\mu_{k+2}}\right)e^{-\sum\ldots}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\bigg\{\eta^{\nu_{1}\mu_{k-2}}\left({\xi_{k-2}}+{\xi_{(k-2)k}}\right)^{\mu_{k-1}}+\eta^{\mu_{k-1}\nu_{1}}\left(-{\xi_{(k-2)k}}-{\xi_{k-1}}\right)^{\mu_{k-2}}+\eta^{\mu_{k-2}\mu_{k-1}}\left({\xi_{k-1}}-{\xi_{k-2}}\right)^{\nu_{1}}\bigg\}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\alpha_{(k-2)k}}\left(\eta^{\mu_{k}\mu_{k-2}}\,\eta^{\mu_{k-1}}{}_{\nu_{2}}-\eta^{\mu_{k}\mu_{k-1}}\,\eta^{\mu_{k-2}}{}_{\nu_{2}}\right)\,e^{-\sum\ldots}

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg\{\eta^{\mu_{k+2}\nu_{2}}\left({\xi_{(k+1)(k-2)}}-{\xi_{k+2}}\right)^{\mu_{k+1}}+\eta^{\nu_{2}\mu_{k+1}}\left({\xi_{k+1}}-{\xi_{(k+1)(k-2)}}\right)^{\mu_{k+2}}+\eta^{\mu_{k+1}\mu_{k+2}}\left({\xi_{k+2}}-{\xi_{k+1}}\right)^{\nu_{2}}\bigg\}

(8.63)

The first term is the trivalent contribution. The next two terms correspond to contact interactions, with
\xi_{(k+1)(k-2)}
and
\xi_{(k-2)k}
being marked respectively. To summarise, we have obtained the appropriate numerator factors as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{T}\left(\prod_{I\in T}\alpha_{I}\right)\hat{C}_{T}\ \exp\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\,\xi_{i}^{2}-\sum_{(ik)}\alpha_{ik}\,\xi_{ik}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{5}\alpha_{(k-2)k}\,\alpha_{(k+1)(k-2)}\ \hat{C}_{T(k)}\right)\exp-\sum_{i=1}^{5}\left(\alpha_{i}\,\xi_{i}^{2}+\alpha_{i(i+2)}\,\xi_{i(i+2)}^{2}\right)
